Yeah… I’m here because I just feel low almost all the time. Most days I wake up and it’s like there’s this gray weight already sitting on me before I’ve even gotten out of bed. I don’t know if I can say that exactly. I mean, over the past few weeks I’ve definitely been low, like I said, but when you ask about “interest,” I kind of get stuck on that word.

It’s not like I made a list in my head of things I used to care about and then noticed, “Oh, I don’t care about those anymore.” I still do things when they come up, or when I’m supposed to, and sometimes I can’t really tell if I’m interested or just doing them because that’s what the day requires. A lot of the time I’m more focused on getting through whatever’s in front of me than checking whether I want to do it.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can really pin that down. I get what you’re asking, like whether there are little things I used to choose to do because they mattered to me and now I just don’t want to bother with them… but I don’t know that I’ve been paying attention to it that clearly.

A lot of my days are kind of automatic. I get up, I do what needs doing, I handle the work that’s in front of me, and then whatever else is expected. So if I don’t do something, I don’t always think, “Oh, I didn’t want to do that because it doesn’t feel worth it anymore.” It’s more like the day just goes by and I realize I didn’t really make room for much else.

There are times when I put things off or don’t feel like starting anything extra, even small stuff, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to do things I used to value. I guess I’m having trouble separating “I don’t want to” from “I’m just worn down” or “I’m just trying to get through what I have to get through.” So… I can’t give you a clean yes on that. I just know things feel heavy, and I’m not always tuned in enough to say whether it’s loss of interest or something else.

I’m not sure I can say it that strongly, like “nothing” feels pleasurable. That’s a hard one for me to answer because I don’t always stop and check, you know? I’m not walking around thinking, “Is this rewarding? Is this enjoyable?” Most days I’m just going from one thing to the next.

There are definitely times where everything feels kind of flat, or like I’m just doing it because it’s there to be done. But whether that means absolutely nothing feels good, even for a little while… I don’t know. I might have a moment here or there where something is okay, or I don’t mind it, but I don’t always register it as pleasure exactly.

So I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in such a clear way that I could say yes, nothing feels rewarding. It’s more like I’m not really tuned into that part of things most of the time.

That’s basically why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities or hobbies that you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know there are things I used to do, and I know I still do some things because they’re part of life, but I don’t know if I can clearly say, “Yeah, I don’t enjoy them anymore,” or if it’s more that I’m just kind of tired and distracted when I’m doing them.\n\nLike, if you asked me whether something feels fun or not, I’d probably have to stop and think about it longer than I used to. I don’t always pay attention to whether I’m enjoying it in the moment. I’m usually just trying to get through the day or do what I’m supposed to do. So… maybe, but I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way where I could give you a straight yes.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities — even ones you used to enjoy or found meaningful?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that exactly. I mean, over the past few weeks I’ve definitely been low, like I said, but when you ask about “interest,” I kind of get stuck on that word.\n\nIt’s not like I made a list in my head of things I used to care about and then noticed, “Oh, I don’t care about those anymore.” I still do things when they come up, or when I’m supposed to, and sometimes I can’t really tell if I’m interested or just doing them because that’s what the day requires. A lot of the time I’m more focused on getting through whatever’s in front of me than checking whether I want to do it.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some of that, but I wouldn’t want to say yes like I’m completely sure. I haven’t noticed it in a clean, obvious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not wanting to do things you used to do, even small things that used to feel worth your time or gave you a sense of purpose?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really pin that down. I get what you’re asking, like whether there are little things I used to choose to do because they mattered to me and now I just don’t want to bother with them… but I don’t know that I’ve been paying attention to it that clearly.\n\nA lot of my days are kind of automatic. I get up, I do what needs doing, I handle the work that’s in front of me, and then whatever else is expected. So if I don’t do something, I don’t always think, “Oh, I didn’t want to do that because it doesn’t feel worth it anymore.” It’s more like the day just goes by and I realize I didn’t really make room for much else.\n\nThere are times when I put things off or don’t feel like starting anything extra, even small stuff, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to do things I used to value. I guess I’m having trouble separating “I don’t want to” from “I’m just worn down” or “I’m just trying to get through what I have to get through.” So… I can’t give you a clean yes on that. I just know things feel heavy, and I’m not always tuned in enough to say whether it’s loss of interest or something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or look forward to — seems pleasurable or rewarding, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that strongly, like “nothing” feels pleasurable. That’s a hard one for me to answer because I don’t always stop and check, you know? I’m not walking around thinking, “Is this rewarding? Is this enjoyable?” Most days I’m just going from one thing to the next.\n\nThere are definitely times where everything feels kind of flat, or like I’m just doing it because it’s there to be done. But whether that means absolutely nothing feels good, even for a little while… I don’t know. I might have a moment here or there where something is okay, or I don’t mind it, but I don’t always register it as pleasure exactly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in such a clear way that I could say yes, nothing feels rewarding. It’s more like I’m not really tuned into that part of things most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel low most of the day, most days. It’s not always because something happened, either. A lot of mornings I wake up and it’s like it’s already there before the day even starts, like this gray weight sitting on me.

I can get up and go to work, be out with the crew, do what I’m supposed to do, talk if I have to. And I can be around my kids and still have that same low, hollow feeling in the background. It doesn’t always show on the outside, I guess, because I’m still moving and doing things, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.

So yes, even when nothing especially bad is happening, it’s still there. It’s kind of like I carry it with me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Very much so. That’s actually one of the things I notice more because it’s physical enough that I can’t really talk myself out of it.

Like, I’ll start the day and I haven’t even done that much yet, and by midmorning my body feels like I’ve already put in a full day. It’s not just normal being tired from work, because I know what that feels like. Landscaping and grounds work, you expect to be worn out if you’ve been hauling stuff around or out in the heat or doing a long day. But this is earlier than that, and sometimes before I’ve really earned it, if that makes sense.

Even simple parts of the day feel like they take more out of me than they should. Loading tools, checking the job plans, getting things organized — stuff I’ve done a thousand times — it feels like I’m dragging myself through it. And then at home, even making dinner can feel like this whole extra job I don’t have the gas for.

It doesn’t always fix itself with sleep, either. Like on a Sunday, if I sleep more or I’m not doing much physically, I still feel heavy and slow. I’ll think, “Okay, I rested, so I should feel better,” but then I’m still moving like I’m carrying something around. So yes, most days there’s this lack of energy that doesn’t really match what I’ve actually done.

I just know things feel heavy, and I’m not always tuned in enough to say whether it’s loss of interest or something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or look forward to — seems pleasurable or rewarding, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that strongly, like “nothing” feels pleasurable. That’s a hard one for me to answer because I don’t always stop and check, you know? I’m not walking around thinking, “Is this rewarding? Is this enjoyable?” Most days I’m just going from one thing to the next.\n\nThere are definitely times where everything feels kind of flat, or like I’m just doing it because it’s there to be done. But whether that means absolutely nothing feels good, even for a little while… I don’t know. I might have a moment here or there where something is okay, or I don’t mind it, but I don’t always register it as pleasure exactly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in such a clear way that I could say yes, nothing feels rewarding. It’s more like I’m not really tuned into that part of things most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel low or down most of the day, nearly every day, even when nothing particularly bad is happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel low most of the day, most days. It’s not always because something happened, either. A lot of mornings I wake up and it’s like it’s already there before the day even starts, like this gray weight sitting on me.\n\nI can get up and go to work, be out with the crew, do what I’m supposed to do, talk if I have to. And I can be around my kids and still have that same low, hollow feeling in the background. It doesn’t always show on the outside, I guess, because I’m still moving and doing things, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing especially bad is happening, it’s still there. I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly. I mean… I’m hard on myself sometimes, sure, but I’ve kind of always been that way. I mean, I know there are things I used to do, and I know I still do some things because they’re part of life, but I don’t know if I can clearly say, “Yeah, I don’t enjoy them anymore,” or if it’s more that I’m just kind of tired and distracted when I’m doing them.\n\nLike, if you asked me whether something feels fun or not, I’d probably have to stop and think about it longer than I used to. I don’t always pay attention to whether I’m enjoying it in the moment. I’m usually just trying to get through the day or do what I’m supposed to do. So… maybe, but I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way where I could give you a straight yes.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities — even ones you used to enjoy or found meaningful?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that exactly. I mean, over the past few weeks I’ve definitely been low, like I said, but when you ask about “interest,” I kind of get stuck on that word.\n\nIt’s not like I made a list in my head of things I used to care about and then noticed, “Oh, I don’t care about those anymore.” I still do things when they come up, or when I’m supposed to, and sometimes I can’t really tell if I’m interested or just doing them because that’s what the day requires. A lot of the time I’m more focused on getting through whatever’s in front of me than checking whether I want to do it.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some of that, but I wouldn’t want to say yes like I’m completely sure. I haven’t noticed it in a clean, obvious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not wanting to do things you used to do, even small things that used to feel worth your time or gave you a sense of purpose?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really pin that down. I get what you’re asking, like whether there are little things I used to choose to do because they mattered to me and now I just don’t want to bother with them… but I don’t know that I’ve been paying attention to it that clearly.\n\nA lot of my days are kind of automatic. I get up, I do what needs doing, I handle the work that’s in front of me, and then whatever else is expected. So if I don’t do something, I don’t always think, “Oh, I didn’t want to do that because it doesn’t feel worth it anymore.” It’s more like the day just goes by and I realize I didn’t really make room for much else.\n\nThere are times when I put things off or don’t feel like starting anything extra, even small stuff, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to do things I used to value. I guess I’m having trouble separating “I don’t want to” from “I’m just worn down” or “I’m just trying to get through what I have to get through.” So… I can’t give you a clean yes on that. I just know things feel heavy, and I’m not always tuned in enough to say whether it’s loss of interest or something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or look forward to — seems pleasurable or rewarding, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that strongly, like “nothing” feels pleasurable. That’s a hard one for me to answer because I don’t always stop and check, you know? I’m not walking around thinking, “Is this rewarding? Is this enjoyable?” Most days I’m just going from one thing to the next.\n\nThere are definitely times where everything feels kind of flat, or like I’m just doing it because it’s there to be done. But whether that means absolutely nothing feels good, even for a little while… I don’t know. I might have a moment here or there where something is okay, or I don’t mind it, but I don’t always register it as pleasure exactly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in such a clear way that I could say yes, nothing feels rewarding. It’s more like I’m not really tuned into that part of things most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel low or down most of the day, nearly every day, even when nothing particularly bad is happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel low most of the day, most days. It’s not always because something happened, either. A lot of mornings I wake up and it’s like it’s already there before the day even starts, like this gray weight sitting on me.\n\nI can get up and go to work, be out with the crew, do what I’m supposed to do, talk if I have to. And I can be around my kids and still have that same low, hollow feeling in the background. It doesn’t always show on the outside, I guess, because I’m still moving and doing things, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing especially bad is happening, it’s still there. It’s kind of like I carry it with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you're feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven't been doing much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. That’s actually one of the things I notice more because it’s physical enough that I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nLike, I’ll start the day and I haven’t even done that much yet, and by midmorning my body feels like I’ve already put in a full day. It’s not just normal being tired from work, because I know what that feels like. Landscaping and grounds work, you expect to be worn out if you’ve been hauling stuff around or out in the heat or doing a long day. But this is earlier than that, and sometimes before I’ve really earned it, if that makes sense.\n\nEven simple parts of the day feel like they take more out of me than they should. Loading tools, checking the job plans, getting things organized — stuff I’ve done a thousand times — it feels like I’m dragging myself through it. And then at home, even making dinner can feel like this whole extra job I don’t have the gas for.\n\nIt doesn’t always fix itself with sleep, either. Like on a Sunday, if I sleep more or I’m not doing much physically, I still feel heavy and slow. I’ll think, “Okay, I rested, so I should feel better,” but then I’m still moving like I’m carrying something around. So yes, most days there’s this lack of energy that doesn’t really match what I’ve actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it doesn’t seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly. I mean… I’m hard on myself sometimes, sure, but I’ve kind of always been that way. If something doesn’t get done right, or I feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll think about it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know if it’s more than usual, or if it’s this big thing where I’m walking around thinking, “I’m a failure.”\n\nThe word “failure” feels… I don’t know, kind of strong. I might think I’m not doing enough, or I should be better at certain things, but I don’t always connect that to blaming myself for everything. Sometimes it’s just this general sense that I’m not quite measuring up, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s because of anything specific or if it even makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear change where I’m blaming myself all the time. It’s more like there are moments where I get down on myself, but I don’t know that it stands out as something new or worse.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{
  "candidates": ["F32.9", "F33.0", "F33.1", "F33.2"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I have days where I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly noticed a concentration problem.

Like, at work I can still follow what needs to be done and make decisions if someone asks me something. I might take a little longer sometimes, or I’ll feel kind of foggy, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to focus. Conversations… I can usually keep up, unless I’m already distracted or tired, but I don’t think people are repeating themselves to me all the time or anything like that.

Making decisions can feel annoying, even small ones, but I’m not sure if that’s because I can’t concentrate or because I just don’t feel like dealing with one more thing. So… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a big, obvious way. It’s not one of the main things I’d point to, if that makes sense.

I go to work, I’m out with the crew doing landscaping and grounds work, and I can talk and do what I’m supposed to do, but inside it feels kind of hollow, like I’m just moving through the day because I have to.\n\nAnd it’s not only when I’m by myself. That’s basically why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities or hobbies that you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know there are things I used to do, and I know I still do some things because they’re part of life, but I don’t know if I can clearly say, “Yeah, I don’t enjoy them anymore,” or if it’s more that I’m just kind of tired and distracted when I’m doing them.\n\nLike, if you asked me whether something feels fun or not, I’d probably have to stop and think about it longer than I used to. I don’t always pay attention to whether I’m enjoying it in the moment. I’m usually just trying to get through the day or do what I’m supposed to do. So… maybe, but I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way where I could give you a straight yes.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities — even ones you used to enjoy or found meaningful?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that exactly. I mean, over the past few weeks I’ve definitely been low, like I said, but when you ask about “interest,” I kind of get stuck on that word.\n\nIt’s not like I made a list in my head of things I used to care about and then noticed, “Oh, I don’t care about those anymore.” I still do things when they come up, or when I’m supposed to, and sometimes I can’t really tell if I’m interested or just doing them because that’s what the day requires. A lot of the time I’m more focused on getting through whatever’s in front of me than checking whether I want to do it.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some of that, but I wouldn’t want to say yes like I’m completely sure. I haven’t noticed it in a clean, obvious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not wanting to do things you used to do, even small things that used to feel worth your time or gave you a sense of purpose?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really pin that down. I get what you’re asking, like whether there are little things I used to choose to do because they mattered to me and now I just don’t want to bother with them… but I don’t know that I’ve been paying attention to it that clearly.\n\nA lot of my days are kind of automatic. I get up, I do what needs doing, I handle the work that’s in front of me, and then whatever else is expected. So if I don’t do something, I don’t always think, “Oh, I didn’t want to do that because it doesn’t feel worth it anymore.” It’s more like the day just goes by and I realize I didn’t really make room for much else.\n\nThere are times when I put things off or don’t feel like starting anything extra, even small stuff, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to do things I used to value. I guess I’m having trouble separating “I don’t want to” from “I’m just worn down” or “I’m just trying to get through what I have to get through.” So… I can’t give you a clean yes on that. I just know things feel heavy, and I’m not always tuned in enough to say whether it’s loss of interest or something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or look forward to — seems pleasurable or rewarding, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that strongly, like “nothing” feels pleasurable. That’s a hard one for me to answer because I don’t always stop and check, you know? I’m not walking around thinking, “Is this rewarding? Is this enjoyable?” Most days I’m just going from one thing to the next.\n\nThere are definitely times where everything feels kind of flat, or like I’m just doing it because it’s there to be done. But whether that means absolutely nothing feels good, even for a little while… I don’t know. I might have a moment here or there where something is okay, or I don’t mind it, but I don’t always register it as pleasure exactly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in such a clear way that I could say yes, nothing feels rewarding. It’s more like I’m not really tuned into that part of things most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel low or down most of the day, nearly every day, even when nothing particularly bad is happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel low most of the day, most days. It’s not always because something happened, either. A lot of mornings I wake up and it’s like it’s already there before the day even starts, like this gray weight sitting on me.\n\nI can get up and go to work, be out with the crew, do what I’m supposed to do, talk if I have to. And I can be around my kids and still have that same low, hollow feeling in the background. It doesn’t always show on the outside, I guess, because I’m still moving and doing things, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing especially bad is happening, it’s still there. It’s kind of like I carry it with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you're feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven't been doing much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. That’s actually one of the things I notice more because it’s physical enough that I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nLike, I’ll start the day and I haven’t even done that much yet, and by midmorning my body feels like I’ve already put in a full day. It’s not just normal being tired from work, because I know what that feels like. Landscaping and grounds work, you expect to be worn out if you’ve been hauling stuff around or out in the heat or doing a long day. But this is earlier than that, and sometimes before I’ve really earned it, if that makes sense.\n\nEven simple parts of the day feel like they take more out of me than they should. Loading tools, checking the job plans, getting things organized — stuff I’ve done a thousand times — it feels like I’m dragging myself through it. And then at home, even making dinner can feel like this whole extra job I don’t have the gas for.\n\nIt doesn’t always fix itself with sleep, either. Like on a Sunday, if I sleep more or I’m not doing much physically, I still feel heavy and slow. I’ll think, “Okay, I rested, so I should feel better,” but then I’m still moving like I’m carrying something around. So yes, most days there’s this lack of energy that doesn’t really match what I’ve actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it doesn’t seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly. I mean… I’m hard on myself sometimes, sure, but I’ve kind of always been that way. If something doesn’t get done right, or I feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll think about it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know if it’s more than usual, or if it’s this big thing where I’m walking around thinking, “I’m a failure.”\n\nThe word “failure” feels… I don’t know, kind of strong. I might think I’m not doing enough, or I should be better at certain things, but I don’t always connect that to blaming myself for everything. Sometimes it’s just this general sense that I’m not quite measuring up, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s because of anything specific or if it even makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear change where I’m blaming myself all the time. I might think I’m not doing enough, or I should be better at certain things, but I don’t always connect that to blaming myself for everything. Sometimes it’s just this general sense that I’m not quite measuring up, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s because of anything specific or if it even makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear change where I’m blaming myself all the time. It’s more like there are moments where I get down on myself, but I don’t know that it stands out as something new or worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder to focus or concentrate on tasks, even simple ones, like following a conversation or making decisions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I have days where I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly noticed a concentration problem.\n\nLike, at work I can still follow what needs to be done and make decisions if someone asks me something. I might take a little longer sometimes, or I’ll feel kind of foggy, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to focus. Conversations… I can usually keep up, unless I’m already distracted or tired, but I don’t think people are repeating themselves to me all the time or anything like that.\n\nMaking decisions can feel annoying, even small ones, but I’m not sure if that’s because I can’t concentrate or because I just don’t feel like dealing with one more thing. So… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a big, obvious way. It’s not one of the main things I’d point to, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you no longer care about things you used to find important or meaningful, even small things that used to give you a sense of purpose or direction?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 10\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

Very much so. That’s actually one of the things I notice more because it’s physical enough that I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nLike, I’ll start the day and I haven’t even done that much yet, and by midmorning my body feels like I’ve already put in a full day. It’s not just normal being tired from work, because I know what that feels like. Landscaping and grounds work, you expect to be worn out if you’ve been hauling stuff around or out in the heat or doing a long day. And then at home, even making dinner can feel like this whole extra job I don’t have the gas for.\n\nIt doesn’t always fix itself with sleep, either. Like on a Sunday, if I sleep more or I’m not doing much physically, I still feel heavy and slow. I’ll think, “Okay, I rested, so I should feel better,” but then I’m still moving like I’m carrying something around. So yes, most days there’s this lack of energy that doesn’t really match what I’ve actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it doesn’t seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly. I mean… I’m hard on myself sometimes, sure, but I’ve kind of always been that way. If something doesn’t get done right, or I feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll think about it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know if it’s more than usual, or if it’s this big thing where I’m walking around thinking, “I’m a failure.”\n\nThe word “failure” feels… I don’t know, kind of strong. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say it that way, exactly. I mean, I still care about things, at least in my head. Like if something matters, I know it matters. Work still matters because it’s my work, and doing a decent job matters to me. Keeping up with things at home matters. Family stuff matters. I don’t feel like I’ve just stopped believing any of that is important.

But I guess sometimes it feels harder to feel it, if that makes sense. Like the meaning of it is there, but it doesn’t always hit me the same way. I’ll do something because I know I’m supposed to, or because it’s the right thing to do, not necessarily because I’m getting that sense of purpose from it in the moment.

So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I no longer care. That sounds too final. It’s more like I still care, but sometimes it feels kind of muted, or distant. I don’t know if that’s what you mean.

Most days I wake up and it’s like there’s this gray weight already sitting on me before I’ve even gotten out of bed. I go to work, I’m out with the crew doing landscaping and grounds work, and I can talk and do what I’m supposed to do, but inside it feels kind of hollow, like I’m just moving through the day because I have to.\n\nAnd it’s not only when I’m by myself. Even when I’m around my kids, or around people I should feel more present with, I can’t really shake it. I’m there, but I don’t feel like I’m really there, if that makes sense. That’s basically why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities or hobbies that you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know there are things I used to do, and I know I still do some things because they’re part of life, but I don’t know if I can clearly say, “Yeah, I don’t enjoy them anymore,” or if it’s more that I’m just kind of tired and distracted when I’m doing them.\n\nLike, if you asked me whether something feels fun or not, I’d probably have to stop and think about it longer than I used to. I don’t always pay attention to whether I’m enjoying it in the moment. I’m usually just trying to get through the day or do what I’m supposed to do. So… maybe, but I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way where I could give you a straight yes.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities — even ones you used to enjoy or found meaningful?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that exactly. I mean, over the past few weeks I’ve definitely been low, like I said, but when you ask about “interest,” I kind of get stuck on that word.\n\nIt’s not like I made a list in my head of things I used to care about and then noticed, “Oh, I don’t care about those anymore.” I still do things when they come up, or when I’m supposed to, and sometimes I can’t really tell if I’m interested or just doing them because that’s what the day requires. A lot of the time I’m more focused on getting through whatever’s in front of me than checking whether I want to do it.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some of that, but I wouldn’t want to say yes like I’m completely sure. I haven’t noticed it in a clean, obvious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not wanting to do things you used to do, even small things that used to feel worth your time or gave you a sense of purpose?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really pin that down. I get what you’re asking, like whether there are little things I used to choose to do because they mattered to me and now I just don’t want to bother with them… but I don’t know that I’ve been paying attention to it that clearly.\n\nA lot of my days are kind of automatic. I get up, I do what needs doing, I handle the work that’s in front of me, and then whatever else is expected. So if I don’t do something, I don’t always think, “Oh, I didn’t want to do that because it doesn’t feel worth it anymore.” It’s more like the day just goes by and I realize I didn’t really make room for much else.\n\nThere are times when I put things off or don’t feel like starting anything extra, even small stuff, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to do things I used to value. I guess I’m having trouble separating “I don’t want to” from “I’m just worn down” or “I’m just trying to get through what I have to get through.” So… I can’t give you a clean yes on that. I just know things feel heavy, and I’m not always tuned in enough to say whether it’s loss of interest or something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or look forward to — seems pleasurable or rewarding, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that strongly, like “nothing” feels pleasurable. That’s a hard one for me to answer because I don’t always stop and check, you know? I’m not walking around thinking, “Is this rewarding? Is this enjoyable?” Most days I’m just going from one thing to the next.\n\nThere are definitely times where everything feels kind of flat, or like I’m just doing it because it’s there to be done. But whether that means absolutely nothing feels good, even for a little while… I don’t know. I might have a moment here or there where something is okay, or I don’t mind it, but I don’t always register it as pleasure exactly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in such a clear way that I could say yes, nothing feels rewarding. It’s more like I’m not really tuned into that part of things most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel low or down most of the day, nearly every day, even when nothing particularly bad is happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel low most of the day, most days. It’s not always because something happened, either. A lot of mornings I wake up and it’s like it’s already there before the day even starts, like this gray weight sitting on me.\n\nI can get up and go to work, be out with the crew, do what I’m supposed to do, talk if I have to. And I can be around my kids and still have that same low, hollow feeling in the background. It doesn’t always show on the outside, I guess, because I’m still moving and doing things, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing especially bad is happening, it’s still there. Loading tools, checking the job plans, getting things organized — stuff I’ve done a thousand times — it feels like I’m dragging myself through it. And then at home, even making dinner can feel like this whole extra job I don’t have the gas for.\n\nIt doesn’t always fix itself with sleep, either. Like on a Sunday, if I sleep more or I’m not doing much physically, I still feel heavy and slow. I’ll think, “Okay, I rested, so I should feel better,” but then I’m still moving like I’m carrying something around. So yes, most days there’s this lack of energy that doesn’t really match what I’ve actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it doesn’t seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly. I’m not sure I can say it was like that, not in the way you’re asking. “Almost all activities” sounds pretty broad, and I don’t know that I’ve had a clear stretch where I enjoyed nothing or had no interest in anything for two weeks straight.

I don’t know if I’d describe it as separate periods with a clean break in between. It hasn’t really felt like, you know, I was down for two weeks, then fine for a while, then down again. It’s been more like one long stretch that just kind of settled in and never fully lifted.

The longer days just sort of took over my life. At first I thought I was just worn out and that I’d adjust, or that it was a bad season and it would pass, but it didn’t really go back to how it was.

There are better days and worse days, sure, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a real break where I felt back to normal before it started again. It’s more like it’s been going on in the background this whole time since that schedule change.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m here because I just feel low almost all the time. Most days I wake up and it’s like there’s this gray weight already sitting on me before I’ve even gotten out of bed. I go to work, I’m out with the crew doing landscaping and grounds work, and I can talk and do what I’m supposed to do, but inside it feels kind of hollow, like I’m just moving through the day because I have to.\n\nAnd it’s not only when I’m by myself. Even when I’m around my kids, or around people I should feel more present with, I can’t really shake it. I’m there, but I don’t feel like I’m really there, if that makes sense. It’s like I’m going through the motions and carrying this heavy, dull feeling everywhere. That’s basically why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities or hobbies that you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know there are things I used to do, and I know I still do some things because they’re part of life, but I don’t know if I can clearly say, “Yeah, I don’t enjoy them anymore,” or if it’s more that I’m just kind of tired and distracted when I’m doing them.\n\nLike, if you asked me whether something feels fun or not, I’d probably have to stop and think about it longer than I used to. I don’t always pay attention to whether I’m enjoying it in the moment. I’m usually just trying to get through the day or do what I’m supposed to do. So… maybe, but I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way where I could give you a straight yes.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you noticed a decrease in your interest in activities — even ones you used to enjoy or found meaningful?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that exactly. I mean, over the past few weeks I’ve definitely been low, like I said, but when you ask about “interest,” I kind of get stuck on that word.\n\nIt’s not like I made a list in my head of things I used to care about and then noticed, “Oh, I don’t care about those anymore.” I still do things when they come up, or when I’m supposed to, and sometimes I can’t really tell if I’m interested or just doing them because that’s what the day requires. A lot of the time I’m more focused on getting through whatever’s in front of me than checking whether I want to do it.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some of that, but I wouldn’t want to say yes like I’m completely sure. I haven’t noticed it in a clean, obvious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not wanting to do things you used to do, even small things that used to feel worth your time or gave you a sense of purpose?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really pin that down. I get what you’re asking, like whether there are little things I used to choose to do because they mattered to me and now I just don’t want to bother with them… but I don’t know that I’ve been paying attention to it that clearly.\n\nA lot of my days are kind of automatic. I get up, I do what needs doing, I handle the work that’s in front of me, and then whatever else is expected. So if I don’t do something, I don’t always think, “Oh, I didn’t want to do that because it doesn’t feel worth it anymore.” It’s more like the day just goes by and I realize I didn’t really make room for much else.\n\nThere are times when I put things off or don’t feel like starting anything extra, even small stuff, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to do things I used to value. I guess I’m having trouble separating “I don’t want to” from “I’m just worn down” or “I’m just trying to get through what I have to get through.” So… I can’t give you a clean yes on that. I just know things feel heavy, and I’m not always tuned in enough to say whether it’s loss of interest or something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or look forward to — seems pleasurable or rewarding, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that strongly, like “nothing” feels pleasurable. That’s a hard one for me to answer because I don’t always stop and check, you know? I’m not walking around thinking, “Is this rewarding? Is this enjoyable?” Most days I’m just going from one thing to the next.\n\nThere are definitely times where everything feels kind of flat, or like I’m just doing it because it’s there to be done. But whether that means absolutely nothing feels good, even for a little while… I don’t know. I might have a moment here or there where something is okay, or I don’t mind it, but I don’t always register it as pleasure exactly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in such a clear way that I could say yes, nothing feels rewarding. It’s more like I’m not really tuned into that part of things most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel low or down most of the day, nearly every day, even when nothing particularly bad is happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel low most of the day, most days. It’s not always because something happened, either. A lot of mornings I wake up and it’s like it’s already there before the day even starts, like this gray weight sitting on me.\n\nI can get up and go to work, be out with the crew, do what I’m supposed to do, talk if I have to. And I can be around my kids and still have that same low, hollow feeling in the background. It doesn’t always show on the outside, I guess, because I’m still moving and doing things, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing especially bad is happening, it’s still there. It’s kind of like I carry it with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you're feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven't been doing much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. That’s actually one of the things I notice more because it’s physical enough that I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nLike, I’ll start the day and I haven’t even done that much yet, and by midmorning my body feels like I’ve already put in a full day. It’s not just normal being tired from work, because I know what that feels like. Landscaping and grounds work, you expect to be worn out if you’ve been hauling stuff around or out in the heat or doing a long day. But this is earlier than that, and sometimes before I’ve really earned it, if that makes sense.\n\nEven simple parts of the day feel like they take more out of me than they should. Loading tools, checking the job plans, getting things organized — stuff I’ve done a thousand times — it feels like I’m dragging myself through it. And then at home, even making dinner can feel like this whole extra job I don’t have the gas for.\n\nIt doesn’t always fix itself with sleep, either. Like on a Sunday, if I sleep more or I’m not doing much physically, I still feel heavy and slow. I’ll think, “Okay, I rested, so I should feel better,” but then I’m still moving like I’m carrying something around. So yes, most days there’s this lack of energy that doesn’t really match what I’ve actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it doesn’t seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly. I mean… I’m hard on myself sometimes, sure, but I’ve kind of always been that way. If something doesn’t get done right, or I feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll think about it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know if it’s more than usual, or if it’s this big thing where I’m walking around thinking, “I’m a failure.”\n\nThe word “failure” feels… I don’t know, kind of strong. I might think I’m not doing enough, or I should be better at certain things, but I don’t always connect that to blaming myself for everything. Sometimes it’s just this general sense that I’m not quite measuring up, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s because of anything specific or if it even makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear change where I’m blaming myself all the time. It’s more like there are moments where I get down on myself, but I don’t know that it stands out as something new or worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder to focus or concentrate on tasks, even simple ones, like following a conversation or making decisions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I have days where I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly noticed a concentration problem.\n\nLike, at work I can still follow what needs to be done and make decisions if someone asks me something. I might take a little longer sometimes, or I’ll feel kind of foggy, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to focus. Conversations… I can usually keep up, unless I’m already distracted or tired, but I don’t think people are repeating themselves to me all the time or anything like that.\n\nMaking decisions can feel annoying, even small ones, but I’m not sure if that’s because I can’t concentrate or because I just don’t feel like dealing with one more thing. So… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a big, obvious way. It’s not one of the main things I’d point to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer care about things you used to find important or meaningful, even small things that used to give you a sense of purpose or direction?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it that way, exactly. I mean, I still care about things, at least in my head. Like if something matters, I know it matters. Work still matters because it’s my work, and doing a decent job matters to me. Keeping up with things at home matters. Family stuff matters. I don’t feel like I’ve just stopped believing any of that is important.\n\nBut I guess sometimes it feels harder to feel it, if that makes sense. Like the meaning of it is there, but it doesn’t always hit me the same way. I’ll do something because I know I’m supposed to, or because it’s the right thing to do, not necessarily because I’m getting that sense of purpose from it in the moment.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I no longer care. That sounds too final. It’s more like I still care, but sometimes it feels kind of muted, or distant. I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities, most days, for at least two weeks at any point in the past?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it was like that, not in the way you’re asking. “Almost all activities” sounds pretty broad, and I don’t know that I’ve had a clear stretch where I enjoyed nothing or had no interest in anything for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, there are definitely times where I don’t feel excited about much, or I’ll put things off because they feel like more effort than they’re worth. But if you’re asking whether there was a specific couple of weeks where basically everything felt pointless or I got no pleasure from anything, I don’t know. I can’t really point to that.\n\nSome days I’ll still get something out of little things, even if it’s not a big happy feeling. Like finishing a job properly, or having a quiet evening, or just getting through what I needed to get through. So no, I guess I haven’t noticed a complete loss of interest or pleasure in nearly everything. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more, and then had a break before another period like that happened again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d describe it as separate periods with a clean break in between. It hasn’t really felt like, you know, I was down for two weeks, then fine for a while, then down again. It’s been more like one long stretch that just kind of settled in and never fully lifted.\n\nIf I think back, it really started around when the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule. That was about three years and… maybe three months ago now. The longer days just sort of took over my life. At first I thought I was just worn out and that I’d adjust, or that it was a bad season and it would pass, but it didn’t really go back to how it was.\n\nThere are better days and worse days, sure, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a real break where I felt back to normal before it started again. It’s more like it’s been going on in the background this whole time since that schedule change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

demographics: Male / 48 / landscaping_or_groundskeeping_worker / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S024\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer care about things you used to find important or meaningful, even small things that used to give you a sense of purpose or direction?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it that way, exactly. I mean, I still care about things, at least in my head. Like if something matters, I know it matters. Work still matters because it’s my work, and doing a decent job matters to me. Keeping up with things at home matters. Family stuff matters. I don’t feel like I’ve just stopped believing any of that is important.\n\nBut I guess sometimes it feels harder to feel it, if that makes sense. Like the meaning of it is there, but it doesn’t always hit me the same way. I’ll do something because I know I’m supposed to, or because it’s the right thing to do, not necessarily because I’m getting that sense of purpose from it in the moment.\n\nSo… I don’t know. But if you’re asking whether there was a specific couple of weeks where basically everything felt pointless or I got no pleasure from anything, I don’t know. I can’t really point to that.\n\nSome days I’ll still get something out of little things, even if it’s not a big happy feeling. Like finishing a job properly, or having a quiet evening, or just getting through what I needed to get through. So no, I guess I haven’t noticed a complete loss of interest or pleasure in nearly everything. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more, and then had a break before another period like that happened again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d describe it as separate periods with a clean break in between. It hasn’t really felt like, you know, I was down for two weeks, then fine for a while, then down again. It’s been more like one long stretch that just kind of settled in and never fully lifted.\n\nIf I think back, it really started around when the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule. That was about three years and… maybe three months ago now. The longer days just sort of took over my life. At first I thought I was just worn out and that I’d adjust, or that it was a bad season and it would pass, but it didn’t really go back to how it was.\n\nThere are better days and worse days, sure, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a real break where I felt back to normal before it started again. It’s more like it’s been going on in the background this whole time since that schedule change.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave there been times when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more at a time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, yes, I think so. It’s hard for me to break it into neat chunks like “this two-week period” or “that two-week period,” because it doesn’t feel that clean. But there have definitely been stretches—longer than two weeks—where I’ve been waking up most days already feeling low.

It’s like before anything even happens, before work or family stuff or whatever, there’s this gray weight sitting on me. I can get up, I can go out with the crew, I can talk to people, I can be around my kids, but underneath it I still feel kind of hollow. Like I’m present, but I’m not really feeling connected to it the way I should be.

So, yeah, if you mean times where I’ve felt down for weeks at a time, that’s happened. It’s not always that I have no interest in anything at all, exactly, but there’s this low feeling that hangs around and makes everything feel like I’m just going through the motions.

I don’t always pay attention to whether I’m enjoying it in the moment. I’m usually just trying to get through the day or do what I’m supposed to do. A lot of the time I’m more focused on getting through whatever’s in front of me than checking whether I want to do it.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some of that, but I wouldn’t want to say yes like I’m completely sure. I haven’t noticed it in a clean, obvious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not wanting to do things you used to do, even small things that used to feel worth your time or gave you a sense of purpose?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really pin that down. I get what you’re asking, like whether there are little things I used to choose to do because they mattered to me and now I just don’t want to bother with them… but I don’t know that I’ve been paying attention to it that clearly.\n\nA lot of my days are kind of automatic. I get up, I do what needs doing, I handle the work that’s in front of me, and then whatever else is expected. So if I don’t do something, I don’t always think, “Oh, I didn’t want to do that because it doesn’t feel worth it anymore.” It’s more like the day just goes by and I realize I didn’t really make room for much else.\n\nThere are times when I put things off or don’t feel like starting anything extra, even small stuff, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to do things I used to value. I guess I’m having trouble separating “I don’t want to” from “I’m just worn down” or “I’m just trying to get through what I have to get through.” So… I can’t give you a clean yes on that. I just know things feel heavy, and I’m not always tuned in enough to say whether it’s loss of interest or something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or look forward to — seems pleasurable or rewarding, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that strongly, like “nothing” feels pleasurable. That’s a hard one for me to answer because I don’t always stop and check, you know? I’m not walking around thinking, “Is this rewarding? Is this enjoyable?” Most days I’m just going from one thing to the next.\n\nThere are definitely times where everything feels kind of flat, or like I’m just doing it because it’s there to be done. But whether that means absolutely nothing feels good, even for a little while… I don’t know. I might have a moment here or there where something is okay, or I don’t mind it, but I don’t always register it as pleasure exactly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in such a clear way that I could say yes, nothing feels rewarding. It’s more like I’m not really tuned into that part of things most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel low or down most of the day, nearly every day, even when nothing particularly bad is happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel low most of the day, most days. It’s not always because something happened, either. A lot of mornings I wake up and it’s like it’s already there before the day even starts, like this gray weight sitting on me.\n\nI can get up and go to work, be out with the crew, do what I’m supposed to do, talk if I have to. And I can be around my kids and still have that same low, hollow feeling in the background. It doesn’t always show on the outside, I guess, because I’m still moving and doing things, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing especially bad is happening, it’s still there. It’s kind of like I carry it with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you're feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven't been doing much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. That’s actually one of the things I notice more because it’s physical enough that I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nLike, I’ll start the day and I haven’t even done that much yet, and by midmorning my body feels like I’ve already put in a full day. It’s not just normal being tired from work, because I know what that feels like. Landscaping and grounds work, you expect to be worn out if you’ve been hauling stuff around or out in the heat or doing a long day. But this is earlier than that, and sometimes before I’ve really earned it, if that makes sense.\n\nEven simple parts of the day feel like they take more out of me than they should. Loading tools, checking the job plans, getting things organized — stuff I’ve done a thousand times — it feels like I’m dragging myself through it. And then at home, even making dinner can feel like this whole extra job I don’t have the gas for.\n\nIt doesn’t always fix itself with sleep, either. Like on a Sunday, if I sleep more or I’m not doing much physically, I still feel heavy and slow. I’ll think, “Okay, I rested, so I should feel better,” but then I’m still moving like I’m carrying something around. So yes, most days there’s this lack of energy that doesn’t really match what I’ve actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it doesn’t seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly. I mean… I’m hard on myself sometimes, sure, but I’ve kind of always been that way. If something doesn’t get done right, or I feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll think about it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know if it’s more than usual, or if it’s this big thing where I’m walking around thinking, “I’m a failure.”\n\nThe word “failure” feels… I don’t know, kind of strong. I might think I’m not doing enough, or I should be better at certain things, but I don’t always connect that to blaming myself for everything. Sometimes it’s just this general sense that I’m not quite measuring up, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s because of anything specific or if it even makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear change where I’m blaming myself all the time. It’s more like there are moments where I get down on myself, but I don’t know that it stands out as something new or worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder to focus or concentrate on tasks, even simple ones, like following a conversation or making decisions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I have days where I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly noticed a concentration problem.\n\nLike, at work I can still follow what needs to be done and make decisions if someone asks me something. I might take a little longer sometimes, or I’ll feel kind of foggy, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to focus. A lot of the time I’m more focused on getting through whatever’s in front of me than checking whether I want to do it.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some of that, but I wouldn’t want to say yes like I’m completely sure. I haven’t noticed it in a clean, obvious way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not wanting to do things you used to do, even small things that used to feel worth your time or gave you a sense of purpose?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can really pin that down. I get what you’re asking, like whether there are little things I used to choose to do because they mattered to me and now I just don’t want to bother with them… but I don’t know that I’ve been paying attention to it that clearly.\n\nA lot of my days are kind of automatic. I get up, I do what needs doing, I handle the work that’s in front of me, and then whatever else is expected. So if I don’t do something, I don’t always think, “Oh, I didn’t want to do that because it doesn’t feel worth it anymore.” It’s more like the day just goes by and I realize I didn’t really make room for much else.\n\nThere are times when I put things off or don’t feel like starting anything extra, even small stuff, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not wanting to do things I used to value. I guess I’m having trouble separating “I don’t want to” from “I’m just worn down” or “I’m just trying to get through what I have to get through.” So… I can’t give you a clean yes on that. I just know things feel heavy, and I’m not always tuned in enough to say whether it’s loss of interest or something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where nothing — not even things you used to enjoy or look forward to — seems pleasurable or rewarding, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that strongly, like “nothing” feels pleasurable. That’s a hard one for me to answer because I don’t always stop and check, you know? I’m not walking around thinking, “Is this rewarding? Is this enjoyable?” Most days I’m just going from one thing to the next.\n\nThere are definitely times where everything feels kind of flat, or like I’m just doing it because it’s there to be done. But whether that means absolutely nothing feels good, even for a little while… I don’t know. I might have a moment here or there where something is okay, or I don’t mind it, but I don’t always register it as pleasure exactly.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in such a clear way that I could say yes, nothing feels rewarding. It’s more like I’m not really tuned into that part of things most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel low or down most of the day, nearly every day, even when nothing particularly bad is happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel low most of the day, most days. It’s not always because something happened, either. A lot of mornings I wake up and it’s like it’s already there before the day even starts, like this gray weight sitting on me.\n\nI can get up and go to work, be out with the crew, do what I’m supposed to do, talk if I have to. And I can be around my kids and still have that same low, hollow feeling in the background. It doesn’t always show on the outside, I guess, because I’m still moving and doing things, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nSo yes, even when nothing especially bad is happening, it’s still there. It’s kind of like I carry it with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you're feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven't been doing much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. That’s actually one of the things I notice more because it’s physical enough that I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nLike, I’ll start the day and I haven’t even done that much yet, and by midmorning my body feels like I’ve already put in a full day. It’s not just normal being tired from work, because I know what that feels like. Landscaping and grounds work, you expect to be worn out if you’ve been hauling stuff around or out in the heat or doing a long day. But this is earlier than that, and sometimes before I’ve really earned it, if that makes sense.\n\nEven simple parts of the day feel like they take more out of me than they should. Loading tools, checking the job plans, getting things organized — stuff I’ve done a thousand times — it feels like I’m dragging myself through it. And then at home, even making dinner can feel like this whole extra job I don’t have the gas for.\n\nIt doesn’t always fix itself with sleep, either. Like on a Sunday, if I sleep more or I’m not doing much physically, I still feel heavy and slow. I’ll think, “Okay, I rested, so I should feel better,” but then I’m still moving like I’m carrying something around. So yes, most days there’s this lack of energy that doesn’t really match what I’ve actually done.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like a failure or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it doesn’t seem to make sense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly. I mean… I’m hard on myself sometimes, sure, but I’ve kind of always been that way. If something doesn’t get done right, or I feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll think about it longer than I probably need to. But I don’t know if it’s more than usual, or if it’s this big thing where I’m walking around thinking, “I’m a failure.”\n\nThe word “failure” feels… I don’t know, kind of strong. I might think I’m not doing enough, or I should be better at certain things, but I don’t always connect that to blaming myself for everything. Sometimes it’s just this general sense that I’m not quite measuring up, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s because of anything specific or if it even makes sense.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear change where I’m blaming myself all the time. It’s more like there are moments where I get down on myself, but I don’t know that it stands out as something new or worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder to focus or concentrate on tasks, even simple ones, like following a conversation or making decisions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I have days where I’m not as sharp as I’d like, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly noticed a concentration problem.\n\nLike, at work I can still follow what needs to be done and make decisions if someone asks me something. I might take a little longer sometimes, or I’ll feel kind of foggy, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to focus. Conversations… I can usually keep up, unless I’m already distracted or tired, but I don’t think people are repeating themselves to me all the time or anything like that.\n\nMaking decisions can feel annoying, even small ones, but I’m not sure if that’s because I can’t concentrate or because I just don’t feel like dealing with one more thing. So… I guess I haven’t noticed it in a big, obvious way. It’s not one of the main things I’d point to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer care about things you used to find important or meaningful, even small things that used to give you a sense of purpose or direction?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it that way, exactly. I mean, I still care about things, at least in my head. Like if something matters, I know it matters. Work still matters because it’s my work, and doing a decent job matters to me. Keeping up with things at home matters. Family stuff matters. I don’t feel like I’ve just stopped believing any of that is important.\n\nBut I guess sometimes it feels harder to feel it, if that makes sense. Like the meaning of it is there, but it doesn’t always hit me the same way. I’ll do something because I know I’m supposed to, or because it’s the right thing to do, not necessarily because I’m getting that sense of purpose from it in the moment.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I no longer care. That sounds too final. It’s more like I still care, but sometimes it feels kind of muted, or distant. I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities, most days, for at least two weeks at any point in the past?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it was like that, not in the way you’re asking. “Almost all activities” sounds pretty broad, and I don’t know that I’ve had a clear stretch where I enjoyed nothing or had no interest in anything for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, there are definitely times where I don’t feel excited about much, or I’ll put things off because they feel like more effort than they’re worth. But if you’re asking whether there was a specific couple of weeks where basically everything felt pointless or I got no pleasure from anything, I don’t know. I can’t really point to that.\n\nSome days I’ll still get something out of little things, even if it’s not a big happy feeling. Like finishing a job properly, or having a quiet evening, or just getting through what I needed to get through. So no, I guess I haven’t noticed a complete loss of interest or pleasure in nearly everything. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more, and then had a break before another period like that happened again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d describe it as separate periods with a clean break in between. It hasn’t really felt like, you know, I was down for two weeks, then fine for a while, then down again. It’s been more like one long stretch that just kind of settled in and never fully lifted.\n\nIf I think back, it really started around when the groundskeeping contract changed our schedule. That was about three years and… maybe three months ago now. The longer days just sort of took over my life. At first I thought I was just worn out and that I’d adjust, or that it was a bad season and it would pass, but it didn’t really go back to how it was.\n\nThere are better days and worse days, sure, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a real break where I felt back to normal before it started again. It’s more like it’s been going on in the background this whole time since that schedule change.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, I think so. Like I’m present, but I’m not really feeling connected to it the way I should be.\n\nSo, yeah, if you mean times where I’ve felt down for weeks at a time, that’s happened.
